Google

This is a digital copy of a book that was preserved for generations on library shelves before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project
to make the world’s books discoverable online.

It has survived long enough for the copyright to expire and the book to enter the public domain. A public domain book is one that was never subject
to copyright or whose legal copyright term has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary country to country. Public domain books
are our gateways to the past, representing a wealth of history, culture and knowledge that’s often difficult to discover.

Marks, notations and other marginalia present in the original volume will appear in this file - a reminder of this book’s long journey from the
publisher to a library and finally to you.

Usage guidelines

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the
public and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive, so in order to keep providing this resource, we have taken steps to
prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on automated querying.

We also ask that you:

+ Make non-commercial use of the files We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for
personal, non-commercial purposes.

+ Refrain from automated querying Do not send automated queries of any sort to Google’s system: If you are conducting research on machine
translation, optical character recognition or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the
use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help.

+ Maintain attribution The Google “watermark” you see on each file is essential for informing people about this project and helping them find
additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it.

+ Keep it legal Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just
because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, that the work is also in the public domain for users in other
countries. Whether a book is still in copyright varies from country to country, and we can’t offer guidance on whether any specific use of
any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book’s appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner
anywhere in the world. Copyright infringement liability can be quite severe.

About Google Book Search

Google’s mission is to organize the world’s information and to make it universally accessible and useful. Google Book Search helps readers
discover the world’s books while helping authors and publishers reach new audiences. You can search through the full text of this book on the web
athttp://books.google.com/

CHAPTER 1

8 1, 1.1. μὲν δή, used, as often, in closing a statement and 3
preparing a transition to the next subject, 11 6, 1111 1.

§ 1—8 5. On the morrow the chief allies met at an early hour at
the entrance to the pavilion of Cyaxarés. While he was dressing to
receive them, the friends of Cyrus came with delegates from the
Cadusians, Hyrcanians and Sacae, and with them Gobryas—all
entreating Cyrus to stay with them. Cyrus, who knew that poor Gada-
las was very much afraid of being deserted by him, pretends to think
that Gadatas does not really wish him to stay, but only professes to do
so αἱ the instance of Hystaspas, who came with him, Gadatas, not
seeing through the humour of Cyrus, vehemently prolests against the
suggestion that he was the mouth-piece of Hystaspas. Hystaspas, keep-
ing up the joke, says that the story he had told Gadatas was that Cyrus
could not go on with the campaign because he had been recalled by his
Sather; he then tells Cyrus to his face that he knew he was longing
to go home to receive the homage of the Persians for his great achieve-
ments, Finally Hystaspas, twitted by Cyrus with a desire to go home,
comforts Gadalas with the assurance that he will not leave, until he
had made Gadatas complete master of the Assyrian king.

§ 1. 1. 3. ἥἧκον...οἱ σύμμαχοι: Cyrus had told Cyaxarés
(v ν 43) that αὔριον mpg δεῦρ' ἐπὶ τὰς σὰς θύρας παρέσονται ol ἐπικαίριοι
i.e. ‘the most important’ representatives of the allies. For the
meaning of θύραι ‘the royal pavilion’, see 1 iii 2.

lL. 4. ἕως ἐκοσμεῖτο, ἀκούων...ἐν τούτῳ, ‘while he was dressing,

also be taken as=cum audiret or licel audivisset. Hartman
(Analecta Xenophontea nova p. 181) leans to the latter meaning,
‘quia sic eo verbo egregie inepta barbari regis supettia petshimging,

§ 6—8 7. While they were thus talking half in fun, half in
earnest, Cyaxarfs had come owt of his lent in gorgeous attire and,
seating himself on a Median throne, requested those present lo give their
opinions severally on the important question whether the war should
be continued or discontinued. The first to speak is the Chief of the
Hyrcanians: * There was no need’ he said *of arguments, when facts
spoke for themselves. The contrast between their own present and past
condition alone proved that their strength lay in union, and that
separation would leave them once more to the mercy of their foe’.

14 NOTES ON VI 27

Cyrene, the most important Hellenic colony in Africa, was
founded B.C. 631 by Battus from the island of Thera (Santorini)
at a spot about ten miles inland. The inhabitants were famous
for their skill in driving: hence Pindar (Pyth. 1v 7) calls their
city εὐάρματον πόλιν, Sophocles in his description of the
chariot-race (Electr. 702) mentions δύο Λέβυες ζυγωτῶν ἁρμάτων
ἐπιστάται, as entering the lists. Aelian (Var. Hist. 2, 26) tells
a story of Anniceris of Cyrene, ὃς ἐπὶ ἱππείᾳ μέγα ἐφρόνει
καὶ ἁρμάτων é\doer, wishing Πλάτωνι ἐπιδείξασθαι τὴν τέχνην.
ζεύξας οὖν τὸ ἅρμα περιήλασεν ἐν ἀκαδημείᾳ δρόμους παμπόλλους
οὕτως ἀκριβῶς φυλάττων τοῦ δρόμου τὸν στοῖχον, ὡς μὴ παραβαί-
νειν τὰς ἁρματοτροχιὰς ἀλλ᾽ ἀεὶ κατ᾽ αὐτῶν ἱέναι. Arrian Tactic.
α. 19, 4 ed. Hercher: τῶν δ᾽ ᾿Ασιανῶν πάλαι μὲν Πέρσαι ἐπήσκη-
σαν τὴν τῶν δρεπανηφόρων τε ἁρμάτων καὶ καταφράκτων ἵππων
διφρείαν ἀπὸ Κύρου ἀρξάμενοι" ἔτι δὲ πρὸ τούτων οἱ σὺν ᾿Αγαμέμ-
νονι “Ἕλληνες καὶ οἱ σὺν Πριάμῳ Τρῶες τὴν τῶν ἀφράκτων" καὶ

8 31—5§ 44. Araspas, one of the favourite officers of Cyrus, who

§ 52—S8 53. The sight of a team of eight horses abreast in the
chariots furnished by Abradatas suggested to Cyrus the idea that a
team of eight yoke of oxen might be made to draw the towers on
wheels, which he expected would stand him in good stead. When these
lowers were finished, he made trial of the draught and found by proof
that eight yoke would draw a tower with all the soldiers upon it
more easily than a single yoke the ordinary load of baggage. Cyrus
therefore arranged to employ these towers in the field of action.

§ 4—8§ 8. Preparations for the war are continued by Cyrus on a
scale of magnificence, as ke was bent upon no mean enterprise. He
carried out the suggestions of his allies, and endeavoured to stir up a
spirit of emulation among his own countrymen in military accom-
Plishments. To this end he employed them in exercises, especially
hunting, and rewarded the most meritorious with prises. He ani-
malted swith praises and indulgences the officers who took most pains
in drilling and disciplining their men. The result was manifest in
the general alacrity and elation of the army (§ 4—§ 5). The Persian
cavalry numbered now 10,000, and there were as many as 300 scythe-
armed chariots (§ 6—3§ 8).

18 11. 71. Αἰγυπτίους: Herodotus (1 77) speaks of Croesus having

l. 73. σὺν ἀσπίσι ποδήρεσι, cum clypeis talaribus, The word
ποδήρη, ‘reaching to the feet’, is applied to a robe in iv 2.

The ordinary Egyptian shields were circular at the top and of
oblong shape, the sides being parallel or contracting as they
descended. They were usually of wood (ξύλιναι, Anab. 1 vill 9)
or wickerwork covered with untanned bull’s hide, having the
hair outwards: they were also strengthened by a broad metal
rim and by a metal boss in the centre of the circular portion.
Sometimes the shield was made nearly the height of the warrior,
who had occasionally to rest one corner of it upon the ground.
In this case a pointed arch was used instead of a circular top.
See the woodcut in Rawlinson’s Hist. of Ancient Egypt, 1 p. 477+
In vil i 33 these shields are said to be far more useful than γέρρα
whether for clearing a way or for the protection of the person.
οἷάπερ καὶ viv ἔχουσι: VII i 33.

posed to be the source of the great wealth possessed by Croesus.

1. 231. χειρομύλας, molas manuarias, *hand-mills’ or ‘querns’,
for grinding wheat or other farinaceous produce as beans, lupins
etc. They were also called in Latin #rusatiles (Aul. Gell, 11 iii 14).

These χειρομύλαι were formed of two stones: the lower
(μὐλος), called by the Romans from its shape meta, rested on
a cylindrical base, from which it rose about two feet high. The
upper and outer stone (ὄνος, cati//us) was shaped like a dice-box
or hour-glass; one half of it fitted like a cap on the conical pro-
jection of the lower stone, at the top of which was i § an
iron pivot, to receive which iron pivot a socket was incavated
between the two hollow cones. The corn was poured into
the hollow cup at the top and descended on to the solid cone
below; where it was ground into flour between the outer and
inner surfaces of the cone and its cap, as the former was turned
round and round by means of a wooden bar. The flour was
received into a channel cut round the base. See the woodcut
representing a mill, found in a baker's shop at Pompeii, in Ricki
Companion εἶσιν p. 427%

CHAPTER III

§ 1—8 4. The sacrifices proving favourable, the march forward
was begun; Cyrus selected his first camping ground as near as
possible to the former, for the convenience of those who might have left
something behind. Cyaxaré did not accompany his nephew, but stayed
behind with a third of the Median contingent. The order of march
was as follows: the cavalry formed the advanced guard, with patrols
pushed out to a distance, and parties of scouts in front ready to creep
up to any commanding position. Behind the cavalry came the baggage
column moving in one or several parallel lines, according to the
nature of the country. In an open country the main body of infontvy <

drought up the rear: in a defile the baggage train was screened ©

either flank by companies of infantry, the baggage of each company
being kept as far as possible together and not allowed to mix with that
of the others, To facilitate their movement, one of the several drivers
attached to each division carried a flag, whick was easily recognisable
by the men of the companies, Thus they marched in a compact body
and strict precautions were taken to prevent any baggage being left
behind.

§ 14—S8 35. Before the first party had reached the scouts, they

were met by Araspas. Cyrus gave him a cordial greeting on his
return, lo lie amazement of those abowt him, who knew nothing of the

=== page 224 ===
viiii rs XENOPHON'S CYROPAEDEIA 45

circumstances of his absence. Explanations follow, and Araspas,
being highly complimented by Cyrus for his public spirit, ends by
being welcomed by all. Araspas then, at the request of Cyrus, gives
the result of his personal observation of the enemy's strength and line
of battle (ἃ 14—8 21). Having listened to the report of Araspas,
Cyrus arranges his plan of tactics, and directs his men lo examine
their own and their horses’ accoutrements; and on the next day to
breakfast early, while he was engaged in sacrificing, that they might
be ready for any emergency. He proceeds to issue his field orders in a
series of exhortations addressed lo each of the principal officers of the
different services. In the first line the cuivassiers were lo be ranged
two deep, twelve in front; behind them the lancers and behind these
the archers, followed by the veteran reserves, who were to stimulate
those in front with encouragement and threats. Behind the main
body the wheeled towers were to follow, each carrying twenty baggage
drivers, and behind the baggage train the women's waggon train:
the longer the line the better, to oblige the enemy ἐσ enlarge their
circuit, if they should attempt to draw a line round them. Behind all
two infantry officers are told to march, each with a company of a
thousand men, while two cavalry officers, with an equal number of
horse, are to keep distinct from the main line, ready to fight first,
if they should be called on. The position of the scythe-chariots to be
settled by lot. The captain of one hundred scythe-chariots to be
stationed in front of the phalanx, the other two lo follow its advance
right and left respectively (§ 22—8§ 35).

§ 12—§ 20. The omens from the sacrifices being favourable, and
the troops having been marshalled according to the directions given,
Cyrus addresses another long exhortation to kis generals. He reminds
them of the advantage which they had over the enemy by their longer
military training together, their previous victory, and the mutual con-

JSidence established between them and their allies, and by their superior
equipment, He concludes with an admonition to them to repeat these
his observations to their men, and to show themselves worthy of com-
mand by setting an example of fearlessmess im word, look and

gesture, and then dismisses them to their posts in the line of battle.

« 8 1—S§ 22. The general officers go to their several divisions.
Cyrus, after taking a hasty meal before he had finished sacrificing,
and distributing meat and drink fo those of his soldiers who appeared
most in need of it, again offers prayer and libation. His staff do
likewise. They then mount on horseback all armed in the same
Jaskion, both men and chargers ; only the armour of Cyrus outshone
that of his staff. A clap of thunder on the right determines the line
of kis march: he starts with Chrysantas and the cavalry on his right
and Arsamas and the infantry on his left. They halt three times,
before coming in sight of the enemy. At last after marching about
two and a half miles, they see the enemy advancing against them, with
his line considerably extended on both wings. They halt, but Cyrus
continues his advance. Dialogue between him and Chrysantas on the
probable meaning of the extension of their wings. His instructions
to Arsamas and Chrysantas as to the plan of attack. The watch-
word. Cyrus, moving about amongst the ranks, delivers himself of
other shorter exhortations and instructions fo officers and men, es-
pecially to Abradatas, Hystaspas, Artagerses and Pharnuchus.

what a long way off (τὸ μέσον) they made the turning point, round
which they wheeled in pushing forward their wings, right and left’.
To understand this passage we must bear in mind that the enemy's
phalanx had halted, while its two extremities which overlapped
Cyrus’ army left the main body and were marching in line at an
angle to the right and left of it; they fetched a wide compass, in
order to avoid coming into contact with the enemy before the centre
advanced. By this movement they presented their flank to the
army of Cyrus, and were too much separated from the centre (πολὺ
ἀπέσπων τὰ κέρατα ἀπὸ τῆς ἑαυτῶν φάλαγγος). But when they had
moved some distance and the main column advanced, they wheeled
about (ἔκαμπτον) right and left, so as to advance in column towards
the flanks of the Persian army,

but Cyrus was prepared for them, he dashed forward with his horse
upon their left flank and closed with them at once. The enemy thus
outflanked beat a hasty retreat. Ariagerses is equally active on the
left with the camel corps, at the sight of which the horses take fright
and general confusion ensues, increased by the invasion of the scythe-
chariots.

§ 20—§ 35. Meanwhile Abradatas with his scythe-armed chariots
“σείει, the centre, and making his way through the chariots charged

the Egyptian phalanx in front. The charioteers nearest him at first
Joined in the onset, but, seeing the firm resistance of the Egyptians,
soon went off in pursuit of the fugitive chariots of the enemy, leaving
none but his personal friends to support him. Abradatas gains a
temporary advantage, but in the general tumult ke and some of his
[friends are thrown out of their chariots, and after a brave resistance
slain by the enemy. The Persians following in the opening made by
kim dealt destruction among the Egyptians, but elsewhere the latter
presented a firm front, having the odds of number and offensive weapons.
Locking their shields together they pushed and thrust their way, while
the Persians, comparatively unprotected by their bucklers, retired
before them foot by fool, giving and taking blows, until they got close
under the wheeled towers, when the Egyptians began to have the
worse.

1, 322. σωθῆναι ἄνδρες ἀγαθοὶ δοκοῦντες εἶναι, ‘to save their
own lives without losing the reputation of brave men’. ‘How can
we’ is their reply ‘save our own lives without losing our reputation
for bravery, since it is a disgrace to surrender?’ ‘We ask you, be-
cause we see you’ rejoined Cyrus ‘standing the brunt of the battle
alone’. ‘The question is, What can we do for the future consis-
tently with our honour also (supposing our valour to be recognised
and vindicated), to save our lives?’ ‘If you save them without be-
traying any of your companions-in-arms, delivering up your arms
and entering into relations of amity with those who might if they
choose put you to the sword but prefer that you should accept
mercy’,

§ 46—8 49. Observations on the victory. On the enemy's side,
the Egyptians were the only combatants who distinguished themselves:
the Persian cavalry and the scythe-armed chariots did most execution.
The camelry did no execution upon the cavalry, as the horses were
Jrightened at the camels, which were therefore never afterwards
employed in field service, but reverted to their proper destination as
mere pack-animals.

CHAPTER II

§ 1—8 8. During the night, while the army of Cyrus were
resting, the enemy were retreating in hasty flight, Croesus towards
Sardis, the rest on their way to their several homes, In the morning
Cyrus pushed on with his army to Sardis. He makes a feint of
attacking it, but in the night sends a detachment of Persians and
Chaldacans to ascend the citadel on the most precipitous and inac-
cessible side by a path leading to it from the river—indicated to him
by a Persian, who had been in the service of one of the native guards-
men—and takes possession of it. The whole city was speedily taken by
storm, and in the morning Cyrus entered it, and, leaving the king
under proper protection, ascended the citadel. Finding that the
Chaldacans had commitled a grave breack of discipline in leaving
their quarters for the purpose of pillage, he indignantly denounced
their officers, and released them from further service, adding threats
as to the treatment they had laid themselves open to. They disgorged
their ill-gotten plunder, and gave it up to the Persians, who had ve-
mained true to their post. The army is lodged in the most convenient
quarters in the cily, with injunctions not to leave them,

8 15—8 29. Croesus, at the request of Cyrus, gives him an
account of his own presumption in putting the credit of the great
oracle of Apollo to the lest, before he consulted him about having
issue ; of the fate of his two sons; of the advice afterwards given him
by the oracle; of his subsequent immunity from misfortunes, until he
was tempted by the King of Assyria to engage in the rash expedition
against Cyrus, from which however he escaped without harm, and
again, misled by his own vanity and ignorance of self and by the
Slattery of others, he courted the disaster which had now befallen him.
He asks Cyrus to give him his opinion whether, now that at last he
has gained self-knowledge, happiness will follow, Cyrus requires time
Jor considering his reply, but in the meantime restores his wife,
children and retinue to the captive king, forbidding him only to think
of war, Croesus says that this generous offer supersedes the necessity
of further reply, since it has made him the happiest of men, and
explains why, Cyrus admired his cheerfulness and for the future
took him with him as his companion wherever he went,

§ 2—8 17. Cyrus is apprised of the death of Abradatas and its
allendant circumstances and of the poignant grief of Pantheia his
wife. He hastens with a guard of honour to the scene of the sorrow
of the bereaved princess, to offer his sympathy and unite with her in
paying the last honours to her husband's remains. On his approach-
ing the bier and grasping the hand of the corpse, it comes away from the
arm, the wrist-bone having been severed by a wound. He delivers the
hand to Pantheia, who refits it to the arm. After offering to send
Pantheia to any place she might choose for her future home, Cyrus
departs. As her husband's body is about to be consigned lo the grave,
Pantheia stabs herself and dies with her head resting on the bosom of
her beloved. The remains of the devoted pair are entombed under a
single lofty monument. [At its foot are still seen the humbler graves
of three favourite eunuchs, who slew themselves by the side of their
mistress.)

CHAPTER 1V

8 1—8 7. The Carians seek the intervention of Cyrus to setile
their internal feuds, He sends Adusius, a Persian, with some forces,
which were augmented by a contingent from Cilicia and Cyprus,
who in consideration of the service thus rendered were granted
certain exemptions for all time. Adusius shows greal address in
reconciling the belligerent factions, taking possession of the strongholds
of both. The two factions become reconciled and unite in begging
him not to leave them and, on his refusal to stay, petition Cyrus to
appoint him their satrap because of the amicable settlement he had
brought about.

81. 1.1, οἱ Kapes: the Carians were included in the Lydian
kingdom of Croesus acc. to Herodotus (1 c. 28). He differs from
Xen. in the name of the general and in his account of the manner
in which they passed under the Persian dominion (c. 171 fii).

l. 2. ἅτε: i 34. It is used when the cause assigned is stated
merely on the writer’s authority.

L 3. ἐν ἐχυροῖς χωρίοιφ, ‘in mountain fastnesses’. A large
part of Caria is mountainous, hence the joke in Aristophanes Aves
293 ὥσπερ ol Κᾶρες μὲν οὖν ἐπὶ λόφων οἰκοῦσιν, dydd’, ἀσφαλείας
οὕνεκα, where there is also a covert allusion to another meaning of
λόφος, viz. ‘crest’; the Carians having been the first to wear crests
on their helmets (Herod. 1 171, Thuc. 1 8),

l. 5. μηχανὰς καὶ κριούς, ‘engines and (in particular) battering-
rams’, ‘battering-rams and other siege engines’, the part being con-
nected to the whole, as often, by καί; cf. 1. 26 Κύρου καὶ Περσῶν.
According to Diod. Sic, X11 28, 3 the battering-ram was first used
in the time of Pericles,

CHAPTER V

§ 1—8 0. Wien Cyrus appeared before Babylon, a rapid survey
soon convinced him that its strong and lofty walls could not be taken by
storm, and he prepared to withdraw his army; but, on hearing from
a deserter that the inhabitants, despising the apparent weakness of
kis force, were going lo allack him, whenever he did so, he increased
the depth, while he diminished the front, of his army, and ordered
them to retire slowly with their face to the enemy, until they were
out of reack of their missiles. They then faced about quickly, and
made several halls of less and less duration, as they got further
away, until they were completely out of danger, when they marched
without stopping until they reached their tents (§ 1—§ 6\.

After they had encamped, Cyrus calls his chief officers together

and states his opinion that an assault on such walls was RE
practicable; the only possible means of taking the RY a

I 12, περὶ πολὺ τεῖχος κυκλουμένοις, ‘since it was a great
extent of wall that they were encompassing’. Cf. ΥἹ iii 30.

Babylon (mod, Hillak), the Grecised form of Babel or
Bab-ili, ‘the gate of Bel’, was the seat of empire of the
Babylonio-Chaldaean kingdom. Herodotus, the earliest
Greek historian who gives any description of the city, which he
visited about a century after its first conquest by Cyrus, says
of it (1 178): —Kbpos, ἐπείτε τὰ πάντα τῆς ἠπείρου ὑποχείρια
ἐποιήσατο, ᾿Ασσυρίοισι ἐπετίθετο. τῆς δὲ ᾿Ασσυρίης ἔστι μέν κου
καὶ ἄλλα πολίσματα μεγάλα πολλά, τὸ δὲ οὐνομαστότατον καὶ
ἰσχυρότατον καὶ ἔνθα ogi, Νίνου (Nineveh) ἀναστάτου γενομένης,
τὰ βασιλήια κατεστήκεε, ἦν Βαβυλών, ἐοῦσα τοιαύτη δή τις πόλις.
κέεται ἐν πεδίῳ μεγάλῳ, μέγαθος ἐοῦσα μέτωπον ἕκαστον εἴκοσι
καὶ ἑκατὸν σταδίων ἐούσης τετραγώνου" οὗτοι στάδιοι τῆς περιόδου
τῆς πόλιος γίγνονται συνάπαντες ὀγδώκοντα καὶ τετρακόσιοι" τὸ
μέν νυν μέγαθος τοσοῦτό ἐστι τοῦ ἄστεος τοῦ Βαβυλωνίου, éxexdo-
μῆτο δὲ ὡς οὐδὲν ἄλλο πόλισμα τῶν ἡμεῖς ἴδμεν. τάφρος μὲν πρῶτά
μιν βαθέα τε καὶ edpéa καὶ πλέη ὕδατος περιθέει, μετὰ δὲ τεῖχος
πεντήκοντα μὲν πηχέων βασιληίων ἐὸν τὸ εὖρος, ὕψος δὲ διηκοσίων
πηχέων. ὁ δὲ βασιλήιος πῆχυς τοῦ μετρίου ἐστὶ πήχεος μέζων τρισὶ
δακτύλοισι. The historian then proceeds to give details of the
building of the wall, how molten bitumen from the village of Is
(mod. 271) was used for mortar and a layer of reeds inserted at
every thirtieth course. Its summit was dotted with low towers

some ro or 15 feet in height, on the outer and the nner edge,
which served as guard-rooms for the defenders. A hundred gates

=== page 280 ===
viv: XENOPHON'S CYROPAEDEIA ΤΟΙ

of bronze—twenty-five in each face—opened into the city, which
was divided into two distinct quarters (¢pdpoea) by the river, and
the roads led straight to these portals, the whole area being thus
cut up into square blocks. Many of the houses were three or four
stories high. There was also an inner enceinte, called Zmgur-Bel,
its outwork bearing the name of Nimitti- Bel, the exact dimensions
of which are nowhere given, but it appears to have been about 44
stades or δὰ miles. This seems to have been the work of Nebu-
chadnezzar; but the great or outer wall was an old work which he
merely repaired and renovated (Rawlinson Z'ransl. Herodotus 1 p.
528).

Ctesias—who was also an eye-witness and the next writer on
the subject, although we have only excerpts from him made by
Diodorus Siculus and others—reduced the circuit of the walls from
480 to 360 stades (Diod. 11 c. 7 περιεβάλετο (ἡ Σεμίραμι5) τεῖχος
τῇ πόλει σταδίων ἑξήκοντα kal τριακοσίων, διειλημμένον πύργοις
πυκνοῖς καὶ μεγάλοις). Cleitarchus, followed by Q. Curtius, made
the circumference 365 stadia, Strabo 385. ‘The two estimates of
Herodotus and Ctesias’, says Rawlinson (Five Great Mon. Vol. 11
p. 511 f.) ‘are respectively the greatest and the least that have
come down to us....It seems to be established, on a weight of
testimony which we rarely possess in such a matter, that the walls
of this great town (which Grote properly describes as ‘artificial
mountains’) were about 40 miles in circumference and enclosed an
area as large as that of the Landgraviat of Hesse-Homburg’,
above 130 square miles.

Besides the great outer and inner walls, two others of brick
skirted the banks of the Euphrates along their whole length
and the quays with which it was lined. Each of these latter
contained twenty-five gates, answering to the number of the
streets they led into. Q. Curtius (XV c. 51) asserts that the
enormous space included within the great outer wall was only
partially occupied with buildings; the rest consisted of gardens,
orchards and even fields, in which a considerable quantity of corn
was grown. According to Herodotus, the height of the wall was
200 royal cubits (more than 335 English feet) and their width s0
royal cubits (more than 83 feet): the spaces between the 40 towers
irregularly dispersed along the wall being broad enough to allow
a four-horse chariot to turn. Ctesias made the height 50 fathoms,
somewhat more than 300 ordinary feet; Cleitarchus and Strabo
estimate it at 73 feet in the time of Alexander, but, no doubt, it
gradually sank from want of repairs, and hence a portion of it
which Xen. (Anab. 11 iv 12) saw, was in his day no more than
100 feet. ‘It must have contained’ says Rawlinson ( Zransl. of
Herodotus 1 Ὁ. 527) ‘nearly twice the cubic contents of the great
wall of China, which is 1200 miles long, from 20 to 25 feet high,
and from 15 to 20 broad’. (Sir H. C. Rawlinson says * wy ows
belief is that the height of the walls of Baliylon ἀνὰ nek wseesh,
6o or 70 English feet’) These dimensions Ὁ = = TY

credible when we read that the ruined wall ot WLESREEEs

north-west portion of the ancient Nineveh, was 130 feel high even
in Xenophon’s time (Anab. 111 iv 10); and the so-called wall of
Media 20 feet broad and a hundred feet high and nearly 70 miles
long (ib. 11 iv 12). Ktesias tells us that the number of labourers
employed in building the wall was one million.

1. 47. οὕτως ἰσχυρὰ καὶ ὑψηλά: see n. to § 2. προσμα-

χόμενος, ‘by assault’.

The broad and deep trenches in front of the walls made it
impossible to undermine them; even if these could be filled up
under the missiles of the enemy in a few places for the battering-
rams to be brought forward, the strength of the walls was so
great that they could not be broken. Still less possible was it
to mount them. They were so high that the arrows of the
besiegers could not reach them with force, and even if the attack
was carried successfully over the trenches, no towers or ladders
would be at once strong and high enough to bring the storming
party to the turrets. DUNCKER, ist. of Antig., v1 p. 86, Eng. Tr.

The stratagem which Cyrus resolved to put into execution
was probably conceived by him at Agbatana before he marched
from that quarter upon Babylon 135 years after the fall of Sardis,

By the dispersion of the waters of the Gyndes (mod, Diydlai), his
army had perhaps gained an important experience, before at-
tempting to deal with the far mightier stream of the Euphrates,
where any accident—the weakness of a flood-gate or the dis-
ruption of a dyke—might not only have disconcerted the scheme

on which the taking of Babylon depended, but have destroyed

a large portion of the Persian army. The exact mode by which
Cyrus drained the stream of its water is uncertain. Herodotus

(1 c. 190) relates that it was by turning the river into the receptacle
excavated by a former queen (Nitocris), when she made the stone
piers of the bridge within the town.......Both he and Yen.

that he entered the city by the channel of the Waghwates A
that he waited for a general festival which was Neely \o INE
attention of the inhabitants. before turning the Seas

=== page 285 ===

106 NOTES ON VII V 10

natural bed. If the sinking of the water had been observed,
his plan would have been frustrated by the closing of the city
water-gates, and his army would have been caught, as Herodotus
expresses it, ‘in a trap’ (ὡς ἐν κύρτῃ). RAWLINSON Transl. of
Herod., Vol. 1 p. 541 f. ed. 3, who adds in a foot-note that
the Nitocris of Herodotus still figures in history upon his sole
authority. She was unrecognised by Berosus and no trace of her
appears in the Inscriptions. Some regard her as the wife of

ebuchadnezzar: but without any proof. Her name is Egyptian.
The ἔλυτρον which Herodotus (1 c. 185) ascribes to her may have
been the extensive reservoir built by Nebuchadnezzar near Se-
pharvaim (Sigpara). The river-walls indeed—one of the chief
defensive works ascribed by Herodotus to his Nitocris—are dis-
tinctly assigned to the reign of Nabonnedus by Berosus (ap.
Joseph. ec. Ap. 1 c. 21, see below ἢ. to 1. 181), and the bricks
which compose them, one and all, bear upon them the name of
that monarch.

the difficulty of which has driven commentators to suspect inter-
polation: see crit. note. εἰ καὶ διαφύγοι, etiamsi dilaberetur:

According to Diodorus (11 ¢. 8), whose authority is Ktesias,
there were two royal quarters in Babylon, one on car side of the
bridge, which joined the two banks of the Euphrates, one of
which protected the east, the other the royal palace proper—the

west side of the city. Herodotus too ( ἃ 181) says hak the
centre of each division of the town was occupied by ἃ lotixess,

=== page 292 ===

In the one stood the palace of the kings, surrounded by a wall
of great strength and size (identified with the modern mound called
Kasr); in the other was the sacred precinct of Jupiter Belus (Bel),
in the middle of which was a tower of solid masonry, Shon whi
were seven other towers, the temple of the god himself being in
the topmost. Berosus (ap. Joseph. c. Apion. c. το, 8) says that
Nebuchadnezzar προσκατεσκεύασε τοῖς πατρικοῖς Busnelots ἕτερα
βασίλεια ἐχόμενα ἐκείνων, ὑπεραέροντα ἀνάστημα (‘in elevation’) καὶ
τὴν πολλὴν πολυτέλειαν, It was the same king also, according to
Berosus, who built the famous hanging garden (τὸν καλούμενον
κρεμαστὸν παράδεισον). Xenophon's ἄκραι are doubtless to be
identified with the buildings mentioned by these three Historians.

§ 37—S8 47. Cyrus wishes to have a permanent establishment
at Babylon. He gives public audience lo an enormous crowd of
suitors. In a council of friends and principal officers, he complains
of the personal inconvenience and unnecessary encroachment on his
time occasioned by so large a number of strangers, and that, if this
went on, ke should have no time for intercourse with his own per-
sonal friends. He requests the opinion of those present on the precept,
which he thought of issuing, that in future all who had a petition
to make should be introduced to him by some of his personal friends.

BOOK VIII

CHAPTER 1

§ 1—8 5. After Cyrus had spoken, Chrysantas next addressed
the assembled Peers and Chieftains. He commends the advice given
by Cyrus as eminently favourable to a continuance of their success;
and dwells further on the vital importance of hearty obedience and
willing deference to law and authority : as submission lo authority is
an essential condition of successful accomplishment, so it must be of
the greatest moment for ensuring permanence of success. He con-
cludes by exhorting his hearers lo be constant in their attendance at the
palace of Cyrus, so that they may be ready with service for any occa-
sion that may arise, since they had all common and united interests.

§1. 1.2. ἀλλά: VIIV 55. 87

L 3. ϑή, ‘dam’, as in vii 1.

LL 5, τε--τε, ‘as’—"so’.

1. 8. ἀφ᾽ dv, ‘the means by which’; τὰ συμβεβουλευμένα being
considered as a starting-point.

1. 9. εὐδαιμονοῦντες διατελοῖμεν : VII ii 20.

§ 9—§ 15. Cyrus next turned his attention to the appointment
of officers for the civil service, such as receivers of revenue, pay-
masters, superintendents of public works etc. He reserved to him-
self, as his own peculiar province, the charge of those whom he
thought desirable to associate with himself in the maintenance of his
authority. His reasons for so doing. He showed great tact in
modelling his distribution of the duties of civil administration after
the military; whereby he secured to himself the requisite amount of
leisure for general superinlendence.

§ 9. 1. 6g. ἀποδεκτῆρες.. δοτῆρες : see Greek Todex s. wo.
γνωστήρ.

136 τ NOTES ON VIII 1 16

ὃ 16—§ 39. Cyrus, desivous of securing the [frequent attendance
of the nobles at his court, adopts various measures for inducing or
compelling habitual absentees to come (§ 16—§ 20). The example which
he set of religious practice, justice, modesty, sobriety and self-control,
produced a refining and elevating influence on those who attended
αἱ his court, where a breack of good manners was unknown (§ 21—
§ 33). Cyrus also set them an example of hunting, riding and other
manly exercises, suck as form the best training for war, and inured
them to hardships and physical discomforts, while he bestowed honours
and privileges on those who distinguished themselves most in these and
other such accomplishments (§ 33—8§ 30).

ὃ 40—8 42. The whole of this passage is enclosed between
brackets as spurious by Hug, who gives, however, no note on the
subject. The sentiments it contains are entirely foreign to those
which we should expect Xenophon to give utterance to, if we may
judge from the celebrated passage in the Oecomomicus (c. 10, 3—9),
in which Ischomachus takes his wife to task for practices which are
here gravely commended by Xenophon’s ideal hero.

CHAPTER 1

§ 1—8 a. The means by which Cyrus won the hearts of his new
subjects. He showed always condescension and sympathy with them
in good and ill fortune.

§1. 1. 5. οὕτω καὶ... ἡγουμένων, ita etiam cos, quos constarel

§ 3—8 6. [He adopted the surest method of ingratiating himself
with his subjects by sharing meat and drink with them. His own
table was served with a superabundant amount of viands, so that
what remained might be distributed, in token of remembrance or love,
among his friends and the various deserving officials about his
court and even among his dependents. He thought it would gratify
his dependents if all their food were first placed on his own table,
Jrom which he would also send portions to his friends, thinking that
such a compliment would give them consequence in the eyes of others.
Not that dishes from the royal table have a mere fanciful superiority
over others ; they arereally superior in flavour, being dressed in a much
superior manner: on the same principle that the arts and industries
are brought to a higher degree of excellence in large towns, where
artisans ean afford to he specialists, than in small ones, where sub-
division of labour is not feasible.

I. 22. διεδίδου : such was the practice of the younger Cyrus
also (see Anab. 1 ix 25, 26) and of the Spartan kings (see Sse.
c. 5, I, de rep. Lac. c. 15, 4).

vir iii : XENOPHON'S CYROPAEDEIA 153

CHAPTER III

§ 1—8§ 18. Having arrived at the full possession of the empire,
Cyrus makes his first procession with due form and ceremony and
stately array. He begins by distributing on the day before dresses of
honour first among the nobles, telling them of his intention to visit in
state those portions of ground that had been chosen and set apart for
the gods, and there offer sacrifice with them. He then distributes
other robes of various colours among the officers of lower rank with
instructions lo make presents of them lo their friends also.

Pheraulas, a commoner, is appointed grand marshal to determine
the places of eack in the procession, in the manner to be settled by
previous arrangement. Cyrus gives him presents of robes and riding-
cloaks for the officers, to prevent any jealousy on their part. The
procession moved out of the palace gates between the body-guard drawn
up in two lines— Persians on the right, allies on the left—and half the
war-chariols on one side, half on the other, in the following order: —

Bulls for sacrifice to Zeus four abreast

Horses for sacrifice to the Sun-God

The sacred Car of Zeus with white horses

The sacred Car of the Sun-God with white horses
Another car with scarlet trappings for the horses
Men carrying fire upon a large altar

CYRUS,

with lofty upright tiara and bandeau round it, a purple vest with
white stripes, crimson trousers, and purple robe of honour, his arms
outside the sleeves ; by his side a charioteer. All prostrated them-
selves before him—a form of homage whick the Persians had never
paid before. When Cyrus's chariot appeared outside the gateway,
the 4000 lancers rode in advance, the 2000 formed a regal escort on
cach side,
300 mace-bearers on horseback bravely accoutred with their javelins
200 saddle horses with striped clothing and gilt reins
2000 Jancers
Chrysantas with the original 10000 mounted infantry
Hystaspas with 10000 cavalry
Datamas with 10000 -.
Gadatas with 10000 »

108 § 11. 1. 81. ταῦροι: Xen. is wrong in attributing to the
Persians burnt offerings of victims, such as were customary in
Greece. Herodotus says expressly (1 132) that when offering
sacrifice they build no altar and kindle no fire, but the victim
is brought to a high place, where after calling on the god they
cut the animal in pieces, and cook the flesh, and lay it afterwards
on the grass. The Magian, who stands by, sings the theopony over
it, and the person who has made the sacrifice carries the flesh away

and uses it for a feast. So Strabo Geogr. xv €. 3, 13 θύουσι ὃ

mace-bearers, to tell them to make their wants known ἐσ certain dele

It was Cyrus's way to give his favourites opportunity of becoming

How Cyrus resented his rudeness.

It is the custom of the King of Persia to march out of his
capital on the Nou Réze or feast of the vernal equinox, attended
by his ministers, nobles and as many of his army as can be
assembled. The ceremonies of the day commence with a review;
and then the tribute and presents of all the rulers and governors
of the different provinces are laid at the foot of the throne, which
is placed in a magnificent tent pitched for the purpose in an open
plain. The king remains in camp several days, which are passed
in joy and festivity. Horse-races are among the amusements;
and the monarch, whose favourite horses generally win, gives
presents to the fortunate riders; he also confers dresses of honour
on all the chief nobles and officers of his government, who give
similar marks of their regard to their servants and dependants.
MALCOLM, Hist. of Persia, Vol. 11 p. 405 ed. 18209.

1. 220. πλὴν, ‘only that’, viI i 24. θύῃ sc. ὁ βασιλεύς.

I. 222. ἐσκήνησαν; VIi 40.
L 223. ἐν τάξει, ‘in their several quarters’.

8 35—S8 50. After their return to Babylon, Pheraulas enter-
tained his newly won friend the Sacan, who had given him his horse,
in his own quarters, and held a conversation with kim on the subject
of happiness, as dependent on the possession of money and property.
He offered to make over the whole of his possessions to the Sacan, on
the condition that he would give him just enough out of them for
his daily wants, treating him as his guest. The offer was accepted
and thenceforth they lived together in perfect amity and undisturbed
happiness.

CHAPTER 1IV

§ 1—8§ 23. Banquet given by Cyrus to a select few of his friends,
with Artabasus, Tigranes, the commander of the Hyrcanian horse,
and Gobryas. The arrangements are left entirely to Gadalas, whe
is commissioned to determine each guest’s place at the royal table.
Precedence depended solely upon merit ; it did not remain unalterable,
but was changed according to circumstances. The custom of thus
arranging guests at the royal table still continued in Xenophon's
time (§ 1—8 5). When dinner was over and Gobryas had warmly
enlogised the charily and generosity of Cyrus, which was even
greater than his military genius, the conversation takes a lively turn.
Hystaspas asks Cyrus, why Chrysantas has been given precedence of
kimself at his table, and, when told the reason, expresses kis intention
to try and imitate the arts by which that officer had ingratiated hime-
self with Cyrus (§ 6—S§ 12). Zhe reply of Gobryas to Cyrus, when
asked whether he had formed a higher opinion of his ἀγαθοὶ φίλοι
than he had at first, when he was sceptical as to finding a husband
among them fit for kis daughter. Hystaspas is pleased with his reply
and Gobryas ends by offering him his daughter in marriage, The
conzersalion turning on matrimony, Chrysantas is promised a suilable

wife; Hystaspas takes the opportunity of raising a laugh al the
expense of Cyrus, whom he rallies on the poverty of his wit and the
ness of his temperament (ἃ 13—8 23).

1. 87. A single τε for καί, by which the second member of

in the manner of καί----δέ,

8 28—8§ 36. The next day Cyrus sends home all the allies who
had volunteered to serve under him. Those who elected to remain
and settle in the country were given lands and houses, which are still
in the possession of their descendants. After the departure of the
allies, the treasure taken at Sardis was divided amongst the army, a
choice portion being reserved for the generals and for Cyrus's staff, and
care being taken that each individual had his fair share. It is
rumoured in some quarters that Cyrus must have kept back for him
sf a considerable portion of the spoil, on hearing which he lakes the

opportunity of lecturing his friends and cthers on the use and abuse
of riches. To prevent further suspicion, he gives an account of, and

that they may be considered as belonging to them all no less than to
himself.

Jriends, and also the high officials, elders and peers. Every Persian

CHAPTER VI

§ 1—S§ 13. During his residence at Babylon, Cyrus vesolved to
appoint satraps over the countries he had conquered. The military
governors of fortresses and the officers in command of the district were
lo be exempt from their control. Reasons for this exemption. He
summoned his chief officers to his presence, to whom he explained what
was to be the position of the future civil governors, and their relation
to the military governors, already appointed, of the conquered countries.
Names of those appointed to the several satrapies of Arabia, Cappadocia,
the two Phrygias, Lydia and Ionia, Caria and Aeolia. Cilicia,
Cyprus, and Paphlagonia were made tributary, but left without
Persian satraps to govern them. The system of administrative
government was to be an exact facsimile of that of the king himself
in all its details, The reciprocal obligations of the civil governors and
the King,

Ker Porter traces some affinity between the series of subjects of
the ancient bas-reliefs in the Palace of the Forty Pillars at Persepolis

the nations at large, and as free gifts from the Persians.

CHAPTER VII

§ 1—8 3. On occasion of his seventh visit to Persia, when he was
now well advanced in years, Cyrus had a vision, in which he was
distinctly forewarned to prepare for his approaching end. He goes to
the hill-tops to sacrifice thank-offerings and prays aloud to Zeus and
the other gods for the happiness of his family, friends and country.

§ 1. 1 1. μάλα δὴ πρεσβύτης: according to the Persica of
Deinon (quoted by Cicero de div. 1, 23, 46) he had reached the age
of 70, after a reign of 30 years.

§ 25. 1. 188. ἴσως, ‘I presume’: vIiG.

1. 191. τῇ γῇ... ἀπόδοτε: see n. to vit iii 5 and cf. Her. 111 τό, 141
Οἷς, Tusc. 1 45, 108: Persae (mortuos) cera circumlitos condunt, ut
quam maxime permancant diuturna corpora, de legg. 11 23, 56:
ac mihi quidem antiquissimum sepulturae genus illud fuisse videtur,
quo apud Xenophontem Cyrus wtitur., Redditur enim lervae corpus
et ila locatum ac situm quasi operimento matris obducitur. Cf. Eur.
Hypsip. fr. 757 ed. Nauck els γῆν φέροντες γῆν, translated by Οἷα.
Tusc. 111 25, 59. Concerning the burial-place of Cyrus, see Zu#rod.
p- xl, Note 64.

The journey from Teheran to Bushire is dignified by the oc-
currence of three groups of architectural or archaeological remains.
The first of these in the order of their locality are the relics of
Pasargadae, the capital of Cyrus the Great, near the small modern
village of Murghab. Still does one of the ruins, which consist of
fragments of palaces and temples and a great stone platform built
out from a hill-top, bear the proud inscription, ‘I am Cyrus, the
King, the Achaemenian,” By far the most interesting, however,
of their number is the mausoleum that once held in a coffin of gold
the remains of the great conqueror himself. On the summit of
seven terraced courses of white marble, 18 feet in height from the
ground, stands the pedimented tomb-chamber, where Alexander
the Great found and saw the body of his famous predecessor.
The second group of remains are the rock-tombs of Darius Hys-
taspes and three other Achaemenian kings in the face of a lofty
cliff at Naksh-i-Rustam, in the valley of the Polvar, 40 miles
north of Shiraz. The third are the cluster of palace-temples,
known to the Greeks as Persepolis, but called by the Persians
Takht-i-Jamshid, sole survival of the royal splendour of Darius
and Xerxes. Zhe Zimes, Jan. 31, 1890.

L 192. τοῦ μιχθῆναι in definitive apposition to τούτου: cf. i 40,
and see G. M7. § 804 ed. ma.

1. 196. ἡδέως dv μοι δοκῶ κοινωνῆσαι, ‘I should gladly, I think,
be incorporated with’, i 40, Hier. c. 7, 3, Oecon. c, 14, 10, G. M7,
§ 220 ed. ma. τοῦ εὐεργετοῦντος : the generalising neuter
for the special τῆς εὐεργετούσης (sc. ys).

8 26. 1. 197. ἀλλὰ γάρ, ‘but (I must conclude) for’,

L 198. ὅθενπερ i.e. from the extremities, hands, feet etc.

l. 202. αἰτοῦμαι ὑμᾶς, parenthetical, Vi 29.

lL 203, μηδεὶς... ἰδέτω: see on VII V 73 a —2

CHAPTER VIII

The concluding Chapter (or Epilogue, as it has been styled)
is a commentary in a bitter vein of sarcasm on the t falling
off of the Persian nation after the time of Cyrus. Its genuine
character was first questioned by L. C. Valckenaer?, the cele-
brated Leyden vy and F. A. Wolf: and most modem
critics are agreed in regarding it as an excrescence on the main
body of the work; though Cobet, Eichler, J. J. Hartman and
others have attempted a vindication of it.

It is to be remarked that in every part of his collective works,
with the exception of this Zpilogue, Xen. speaks in terms of
respect of the Persian government and people. In the previous
text of the Cyropaedeia the praises of the political system in-
augurated by Cyrus are so expressed as to apply equally to the
past and the present, and some of his institutions are specified as
still maintaining their ground. Yet these very institutions are
described in the Zpilogue as extinct or superseded by gross
abuses; and it is not easy to comprehend how Xenophon, after
rounding off so well his epic narrative, could have so deliberatel
defeated his own object, by representing the institutions whi
their heroic founder had spent his life in establishing, as destitute
of all organic bond of cohesion, and falling to pieces on removal
of his personal influence.

These considerations, however, are not entirely conclusive
evidence against the authorship of Xenophon which, it must be
remembered, was never disputed by native critics? It is possible

1 ad Mem. p. 238 ed. Ernesti.
* Mure Hist. Gr. Lit, ν τ. 413 fi.

=== page 376 ===
vir viii « XENOPHON'S CYROPAEDEIA 197

that the Cyropaedeia was originally composed in its epic unity,
at a time when the author still retained his early friendly feeling
towards Persia and her institutions; and that in his old age this
feeling, from whatever cause, had given place to bitter animosity,
under the influence of which the Zpilogue was composed, as a
sort of palinody. Yet it is strange that Xenophon, if he was
really the author, should have spoken disparagingly of the morals
of the Persians themselves only, and said nothing about the de-
generacy of the rulers who succeeded Cyrus, and their lack of
capacity for government—that divine gift upon which he elsewhere
dwells with so much fondness. It is also remarkable that the
writer does not appeal to the previous text of the Cyropaedeia®.

There are, indeed, several palpable discrepancies between the
Epilogue and the previous text. Thus compare § 9 τὸ δ᾽ ἐκπο-
νεῖν οὐδαμοῦ ἐπιτηδεύεται with vi il 16; § 9 μονοσιτεῖν
νόμιμον with the many passages which contain mention of the
two daily meals (ἄριστον and δεῖπνον) customary with the Greeks
(the exception in I ii 11 only proves the rule); § 12 οὔτ᾽ αὐτοὶ
ἐξῇσαν οὔτε τοὺς ἄλλους ἐξῆγον ἐπὶ Tas θήρας with VIX
1.34-.-.36; 813 τὰ ἱππικὰ... ἀπέσβηκε (see also § 19) with τν
iii 23. There is Bothing very remarkable about the language of
the Zpilogue, except in the use of the particle μήν, which occurs
so frequently in it, especially in the combination γε μήνϑ.

As to the date of the composition of the Epilogue, it cannot
be put before B.C. 362/1, because mention is made in § 4 of two
events which we know from Diodorus took place in that year;
nor after B.C. 330, otherwise we should have expected some
allusion to the destruction of the Persian Empire by Alexander.

143 Rheomithres, who had joined in the revolt, was sent by his

The numbers affixed to words denote respectively :—

1 doubtful or suspected words

other writers

Der Commentar ist sorgfiltiz gearbeitet und es muss als verdienstlich be-

Within the memory of middle-aged men Xenophon's Cyropasdeia was much
more read than it is now. But as improved editions of the Anabasis multiplied,
the Cyropaedeia, on which Xenophon doubtless prided himself much more than
he did on the Anabasis, has been crowded out of the field, and we are glad to see
that Dr Holden has come to the rescue of a work in which Xenophon often appears
at his best, as he seldom does in the Anadasis. An elaborate introduction in which
contributions have been levied on the best and most recent authorities, abundant
references to Goodwin and Hadley and Allen in the commentary, valuable comments
on idiom and sphere of use, a critical appendix and two indices, make this edition
of Xenophon's Cyropaedeia, Books 1 11 (Cambridge, University Press, 1887)
worthy of the companionship of the Hrero and the Oeconomicus by the same
Editor, and we are glad to learn from the preface that these two are only
the Precursors of a complete edition, American Journal of Philology, Vol. vii,
3}. 387.

PLUTARCH’S LIFE OF TIMOLEON. With Intro-
duction Notes and Lexicon, Extra fcap. 8vo. ὅς,

“Dr Holden's editions of several of the Lives of Plutarch are well
and have won the applause of all competent judges, We are glad to note the
appearance of another of the same series, and to be able to say that no falling
from the high level of the others is discernible. Here again is the same
scholarship, the same lavish learning, the same knowledge of the Greek
guage in every period of its existence, so long as there are any authors init
worth reading. And what life of any hero of history or romance is more
fascinating than that of Timoleon, especially as portrayed by the ring
panel of Plutarch? The terrible and most dramatic story of the φ'

‘imophanes, his mother's curse, and the remorse of Timoleon, form a :

historians were too li to lay on over-freely, it is a romance which is true.

“We have a masterly Introduction, dealing with the primary sources of
information for the Life of Timoleon accessible to Plutarch, then with the
secondary sources....A map of Sicily, a chronological table of events in that
island, and a map of Syracuse find a place between the Introduction and the
Text. The notes, it is needless to say, exhibit sound scholarship and afford the
student just that aid which he requires, and enable him to perfectly grasp the
style of Plutarch, of which the Life of 7Timoleon is an easy and lucid A
A critical Appendix, several Indices and a praiseworthy Lexical Index to the
Life, bring us to the end of a work which will increase the high reputation of the
author." — Educational Times, Dec, 1, 188g.

The Smaller Cambridge Bible for Schools,

The 8maller Cambridge Bible for Schools τοῦ" form an entirely
new series of commentaries on some selected books of the Bible. It is expected
that they will be prepared for the most part by the Editors of the larger
series (The Cambridge Bible for Schools and Colleges). The volumes
will be issued at a low price, and will be suitable to the requirements of
preparatory and elementary schools.
